# Changelog

The same release notes shown in the game (press **N** or open settings).

## v2.30 — 2026-10-26

- Vael’s sky stones are no longer one flat colour where you walk: the plateaus, the great table, the rose cliff, the floating island, the rock arch and the stones you hop across have soft patches of a warmer and a cooler tone, a fine crackled crust, long cracks and pebbles with their shadows, while their cliffs keep their bands. The aqueducts and the monastery’s walls are built of large pale stone blocks, the aqueducts’ decks laid in flagstones.

## v2.29 — 2026-10-26

- The Moon Foundry’s old polishing drum loses the riveted grid that broke up on its curve, and the Buried Machine’s canyon floor keeps its soft drifts and stones further off.
- The open ground in Vael, the Glass Dunes and at home is no longer one flat colour: patches of a paler sandy ochre with a fine broken edge, a few rosier ones, small pebbles with their shadows and short wind dashes near you, stones further off, and still a calm flat colour toward the horizon.
- Viridel’s runnels, once you clear them, look like the builders’ channels: calm flow lines along the water, a pale line of light along each edge and a darker wet lip.
- The Desert’s far dome city is built of curved panels set in rings that follow each dome, with raised seams, a few panels of another shade, small round vents, a band of teal or orange round some domes and sand-scour near the ground.
- The city under the Glass Dunes is banded and scoured by the wind, with a line of sand along the foot of its walls.
- Lorn’s Hush-House and Lamp-House are built of large soft-edged stone blocks, each its own shade, with fine rain streaks and a darker damp band at the foot; the Hush-House’s terrace is laid in flagstones of the same stone.
- The Underwater City’s walls show the sea: white salt at their foot and under the ledges, a few tide lines a shade darker, green stains low on the shaded side, and patches of a fresher pink where they were mended.
- His own clothes, the coral shirt, are calmer, like the other outfits: broad light and shade where many small blotches and specks were, and plain colour on the shirt, tee and trousers. Same cut, same colours.
- Eight of the new outfits look more like their drawings: the storm courier’s cream collar and rolled cuffs, a bigger hood and grey trousers; the furnace mechanic’s narrower bib, longer sleeves and a bigger tool apron; the frost surveyor’s softer quilting and coral ends at the throat; the observatory apprentice’s strap across the chest to the telescope and clean cream cuffs; the festival traveller’s large patches and broad sash; the gravity ranger’s and void navigator’s lighter trousers; the void navigator’s plain harness and grey knee panels; the star archive keeper’s gold inlays.
- In conversations people stand up straight: nodding along or talking with their hands, nobody leans far back with their face to the sky any more. The traveller listening to Plumb looks at it, not over it.
- Viridel’s tea terraces, on the white builders’ steps, are paved in clean flagstones like the cities’ plazas.
- Open ground holds up further off: soft drifts of a paler ochre, long pen lines along the slopes and stones with their little shadows still read out to a couple of hundred metres. In Vael, the Glass Dunes, the Buried Machine and at home.
- Worked timber looks like wood: planks with grain, knots and nail holes, sun-bleached on top and darker underneath, now and then a faded teal plank. On the desert’s wells, camps and landmarks, the City-Shaft’s quarters, the Moon Foundry’s stairs, the Underwater City’s posts and Lorn’s walkways.
- The Moon Foundry’s machinery and block houses are riveted sheet metal now: panels in courses with rows of rivets along the seams, a few repainted another colour, thin rust streaks running down from the bolts.
- The shops and rooms look lived in: their floors are hand-laid tiles with a few of another pastel colour, and their walls are painted plaster over a darker band at waist height and a tiled skirting, in place of ruled grids. In every world’s shop and every room.

## v2.28 — 2026-10-25

- The makers’ houses look machined: every wall panel and floor plate has its own tone, some panels sit set back in shadow, and floor plates carry an engraved border a hand in from their joints, small round sockets and, up close, fine machining marks. In twelve of the thirteen houses (the Rose House keeps its red stone).
- The paved plazas and streets are laid in flagstones now instead of a ruled grid: courses of uneven stones with worn corners, each a shade lighter, rosier or sandier, the odd cracked slab, grit in the joints near your feet and a brass survey disc here and there. Far off the joints thin out to flat colour. On the City-Shaft’s rim, the Signal Market’s floor and pavements, the Moon Foundry’s yard, the piers and bridges of the City Floating in Space, the Underwater City’s streets and the Garden of Spheres’ paths.
- In the browser version, turning the camera round near where you land no longer freezes the game for a moment in several worlds. The pieces of the makers’ runs and fight-temple doors close by (the desert’s Wind hall, Vael’s Bell crossing, the Glass Dunes’ Disc run and others) are now prepared behind the loading screen instead of the first time you look at them, and the scenery no longer sends all of itself to the graphics card again the first time a far building changes its level of detail, just after you arrive.
- The other twelve makers’ houses now end like the Rose House and the Aerie: walking through the guardian hall’s door plays a short introduction showing its guardian and what is wrong in its hall, then the guardian wakes and the challenge begins. If you are knocked out, walk back in to try again without watching it again. Leave the hall another way, or reload, and the guardian settles, with nothing lost. Winning still plays the house’s ending and changes the world.
- In the desert, the fragment of the singing light at the cairn beside your ship can now be heard from the foot of the ramp and from where Marrow stands, a faint song behind you as you set off for the city. Take it and the next one along the pilgrims’ road starts singing from there.
- Sounds that answer one blow no longer pile up: when a swing meets several guarded or armoured foes at once you hear one clang, not three stacked into a crash, and the engine shards cutting a crowd sound one slash per pass. Each clang is pitched a little differently, so a combo on a guard no longer repeats the same note. When someone speaks, the wind, a storm and the rain step back for their line, and the world’s occasional horn or croak waits until they finish. His breath when he lands a small hop is a little softer, and it and the pickup sound now differ slightly each time.
- Plumb and 32 other characters now speak plainly. Plumb’s help when you’re lost, in the makers’ houses and against each guardian, and its words on the ship after the crash, on the map and against the pirates, now say what to do and where, without riddles. People’s directions and the guardians’ fight notices do too.
- The last two house maps are on show like the others: bigger, on a low plinth, their backs to the wall and their openings toward the room. In the City-Shaft’s Vane Room it stands against the wall of the ledge, north of the door, leaving the ledge clear; in the Mooring-House’s Lamp Shaft it stands on a floor plate by the wall, left of the way in.
- When a chest opens, what it held floats over your open hand again where you can see it. From some angles your own body hid it through the whole card; now you hold it out in the hand on the camera’s side.
- The Engine-House has a Fuse Room north of the Governor Room: a bomb wakes its clock, and only while the clock runs does the wall panel show which floor tiles to step, and in what order. Out of time, the tiles go dark and you can start again.
- The Warden’s Well has a Wind Clock east off the gallery: step the glyph tiles in the order the wall panel shows, then gust the clock with the fan and run, and a bridge of light crosses the chasm while its lamps last. It never sinks under your feet.
- The Hush-House has a new side room off the Choir’s floor: splash the eye on its pedestal and the lamps set in the floor light the way round a stone screen to a vault door, then go out one by one from the eye, ticking faster near the end. Reach the door before they do; miss it and just splash the eye again.

## v2.27 — 2026-10-25

- Four makers’ houses now give a spell when their guardian is resolved: the Aerie a gold ward that takes two blows, the Whale-House a slow tide that slows foes inside it, the Engine-House shards that circle you and cut, the Mooring-House a veil that makes foes lose you. Press LB / L1 + RB / R1 together (Y on the keyboard, ✺ on touch) to cast; it recovers in 30 seconds, shown by the chip under the gadget’s. Choose your spell in Items. Saves that already resolved those houses have their spells.
- Nine outfits are now won at the games in the worlds: each host says what their game’s prize is, its start card names it, and the first run that wins it shows the new outfit on the results card. The Wardrobe tells you where to win each one you haven’t got. An outfit you were already wearing stays yours.
- Enemies now arrive out of a swirling cloud of darkness: the body grows out of the ink, and they only start to fight once the cloud has cleared, so their first attack is never hidden in it.
- Enemy attacks now leave a swoosh behind the claw, fist or blade that swings: a thin ink stroke while they wind up, a wide warm orange ribbon through the dangerous part of the blow, then it thins away as they recover.
- In the desert, Nour now thanks you for lighting Qanat’s tree before you saw to your own ship, instead of saying you asked for nothing after you had asked her for power.
- Foes now visibly reel back off your shield: a perfect parry throws them off balance and holds them leaning while they are open, and a plain block knocks them back a step.
- The dark spot under you while you jump or fall is now easier to read: a solid ink-edged shadow that shows clearly indoors and in caves, sits on lifts and other moving floors you are about to land on, and stops at a ledge’s edge instead of hanging over the drop.
- When you slow down near someone your quest needs you to talk to, or something it needs you to take, Plumb comes out and hovers beside it for a moment. It keeps out of fights, conversations and scenes, and goes back when you run.
- A new enemy, the makers’ fencer, fights the way you do: an ivory machine with a kite shield and a sword. Its shield turns your combo from the front, and if you hide behind your own guard it bashes it aside. Side hop its overhead, back flip its sweep, break its shield with a charged cut or slip behind it. Parry its sword perfectly and the sword flies out of its hand: it fights with the shield alone while it runs to pick the sword up, and that is your moment. Its leap slam hangs in the air for a second before it comes down. Meet it in the Moon Foundry, the City Floating in Space and the Signal Market, or in the Arena.
- A perfect dodge now asks you to read the blow: wide sweeps are answered by the back flip, overheads and thrusts by the side hop. Only the right hop starts the slow-motion flurry; the other still dodges.
- Some enemies now defend themselves, and each has its own way in. The furnace brute raises its forearms between blows: light cuts ring off, a charged cut smashes through and leaves it reeling open for a moment. The shellback crab clamps down flat in its shell when you close in: come down on it with an air cut and it flips onto its back. The shade turns your cuts with its sword: parry the cut it answers with, then riposte. Hammering a guard with the wrong move gets you a counterblow, never a stalemate, and going round their side still works.

## v2.26 — 2026-10-25

- Abel explains how to guide the mirrors’ beam to the square below, and Sel explains how to light the three bulbs and where to play the cylinder.

## v2.25 — 2026-10-25

- Joss explains which anchor to pull with your tether, and Hobb explains how the makers’ hourglass keeps and plays back a note.
- The Seed Vault, Casting Stair and Choir maps now stand on low plinths aligned with the floor tiles, with their backs toward the nearest wall and their openings facing into the room.

## v2.24 — 2026-10-25

- The Founders’ Belfry porch now has solid floor through its doorway, so you can walk into the Hall of Echoes and back without dropping through a gap.

## v2.23 — 2026-10-24

- Achille explains where to stand and how to use the spring boots to send a deep note through the Whale-House’s dishes.

## v2.22 — 2026-10-24

- The Lamp-House, Clock-House and Footprint maps stand on low plinths aligned with the floor tiles, with each chest’s back toward the nearest wall and its opening facing into the room.

## v2.21 — 2026-10-24

- Conversations use a compact dark panel, a small portrait and plain replies, with a brass diamond marking the selected answer.

## v2.20 — 2026-10-24

- Teasel explains which crystals to quiet in the Hush-House, and Briony explains how to bring light to the Lamp-House’s moth.
- Nearby fragments take turns singing, pause their calls while you talk or read a menu, and leave a little room for the sound of a discovery.
- Kamal now looks the same on his fan sled as he does beside the canyon.

## v2.19 — 2026-10-24

- Hints and quest notices have compact charcoal panels, fine cream borders and turquoise accents. Controls keep their ivory badges, and larger text has room to wrap.
- The Unity opening now shows your father’s hologram with the correct model after updating the app.
- Quest notices and control hints now use clearer left-aligned text beside a separate icon, with more breathing room and a small brass detail on quest panels.

## v2.18 — 2026-10-24

- The Engine-House, Whale-House and Founders’ Belfry now display their map chests on low plinths, away from the room corners.
- The six raised map chests now line up with the floor tiles where present, with their backs toward the nearest room wall and their fronts facing the room.

## v2.17 — 2026-10-24

- Wim points you toward the Clock-House’s boomerang, and Tessa explains how to answer the Footprint’s singing spheres.
- The ship’s windows catch a pale reflection from inside, while keeping the view outside clear.

## v2.16 — 2026-10-24

- The moorers’ court has room to try different tools: lower a crate with the hook, carry it, or push the spare onto the load plate. Its gate stays open after solving it, including after reloading, and lost crates return safely.

## v2.15 — 2026-10-24

- Map chests in the Rose House, Lantern Market and Aerie are larger and stand on low plinths, with more room to approach and walk around them.

## v2.14 — 2026-10-24

- The Keeper and Elder keep their familiar appearance when their temple scenes lead into the water and flight games.

## v2.13 — 2026-10-23

- The Aerie’s final doorway now leads into the Elder’s introduction and flight. Reach the Old Roost to return automatically, with the Elder fully framed and the wind returning in the completion scene.
- The Rose House’s final doorway now leads straight into the Keeper’s opening scene and Cistern challenge. Finish the water route to return automatically for the house’s completion scene.

## v2.12 — 2026-10-23

- Fisk and Petra explain the Engine-House vents and Casting-House controls more clearly, including what to do when the Founder pauses.
- New releases also build the Unity downloads for Android and Steam Deck, and keep them with the version they were built for.

## v2.11 — 2026-10-23

- The ship’s bunk shelf is firmly supported, its folding porch has solid edges, and its room walls no longer disappear as you step away.

## v2.10 — 2026-10-23

- Plumb’s temple hints and several local directions now say more clearly what to look for and try, while keeping each speaker’s character.

## v2.09 — 2026-10-23

- A sealed chest offers inspection, then offers opening when it is ready.
- Use prompts follow what you can see. People, loose objects and chests behind a wall no longer take the button from a reachable target.

## v2.08 — 2026-10-23

- Scene captions appear with their filmed moment, even when a newly entered place is waiting to show its name.
- The Keeper and Elder now introduce their requests in short scenes. Helping them celebrates the water and wind returning to their temples.

## v2.07 — 2026-10-23

- Steam Deck updates can keep arriving without filling up the old download channel. Existing installations keep their saves and update through the same game site.

## v2.06 — 2026-10-23

- The seed pouch has been removed, so walking no longer leaves a trail of flowers. The bloom gun still grows flowers where its shots land.

## v2.05 — 2026-10-23

- Plumb points you toward people and landmarks to explore without naming people you have not learned about yet.
- Flying with the Elder now shows your progress toward the Roost and how to return to her wing when you drift away.
- Plan the Cistern’s channels at your own pace, see the connected route and possible spills, then open the valve. A spill lets you adjust the same board and try again.
- Help the Rose House Keeper guide water and fly beside the Aerie Elder to the Old Roost. Their completed games now open the temple’s way onward, with a safe return if you leave to try again.

## v2.04 — 2026-10-23

- The spring boots explain their short hop inside temples and how to jump, then stomp, to sound the Whale-House’s deep notes.
- Rising water in the Whale-House now carries you up to swim and climb out on native players, instead of leaving you walking below the surface.
- The spring-boots instructions fit their item card while keeping the charge, temple-hop and stomp controls together.
- Sword strikes now show the result they actually achieved: armour keeps its sparks, passing through a phased foe does not look like a hit, and overhead hits recoil away from the blow.
- Looking around before stepping off your hoverbike or ending a flight keeps the camera at the angle you chose.
- A dodge pressed just before a sword cut ends now waits for the first safe moment, in ordinary fights as well as fight temples.
- Small stick movements now take small steps. Jumps forgive a slightly late press at an edge, or remember an unused press just before landing.
- Every temple shares a quiet new ambience, and ordinary doors reuse the same sliding sounds as temple doors.
- Hoverbikes glide more quietly, holograms wake with a warm CRT sound, and chests and keys open with longer magical flourishes.
- A new sound-effects page lets you browse every effect, try its variants and see which sounds are recordings and which are made by the game.

## v2.00 — 2026-10-22

- Sound effects finish loading before play, so assigned recordings keep their chosen sound instead of turning into a substitute. A missing download offers Retry without losing your save.

## v1.99 — 2026-10-22

- Steam Deck players can update the player from the Updates page, with clear download progress and a checked restart that keeps saves and the previous working player.

## v1.98 — 2026-10-22

- A Sound effects switch in the developer controls shows recent effects and playing loops, with their ElevenLabs, procedural or other recording source, to help spot overlapping sounds.

## v1.97 — 2026-10-22

- New voicemail, ship controls, small pickups and newly charted worlds now have warm, rounded sound cues.
- Forming the fluid blade, opening the shield and refilling the tank have softer new sounds, with a discreet reminder when fluid runs low.

## v1.96 — 2026-10-22

- The galactic map fits the native screen when its size settles or changes.
- Backpack jets no longer reignite when you switch between aiming and forward thrust.
- Footsteps land cleanly without extra landing taps or trailing sand scrapes.
- Temple sliding doors, mechanisms, treasure reveals and completed quests now share a new set of sounds across every house.
- The traveller now has quieter, distinct efforts for fighting, jumping, landing and climbing, with an occasional breath after a sustained run.
- The camera stays closer while gliding inside the Aerie, making the Traveller and landing ledges easier to read.

## v1.95 — 2026-10-21

- Advice stays with Plumb until you ask. Automatic hint boxes no longer interrupt your journey; important notices and conversations remain.
- Plumb now speaks for the ship. Messages wait their turn instead of appearing together.
- Kamal now explains his claim to the salvaged hoverbike and races on his own fan sled. Win to keep the bike and your backpack; a loss leaves a rematch to settle the wager.

## v1.94 — 2026-10-21

- The Rose House’s sliding doors carry their glowing circuits and stone pattern with them.
- The Rose House entrance now joins the building instead of standing apart from it.
- Jumping forward while locked on now carries you toward your enemy, even from a standing start.
- Your feet stay planted as the Moon Foundry’s moving platforms rise and descend.

## v1.93 — 2026-10-21

- The selected recorded effects now play in native versions as well as the browser.
- The folding deck has distinct opening and closing sounds that follow its movement and locking finish.
- The ship has a warm cabin hum and a shared booster sound for travel, landings and Chime Pirates.
- Boots settle softly into fine sand, compress grass and tap stone with separate footsteps.
- The fluid gun fires a smaller, rounded energy blip with a playful liquid finish.
- Your energy blade now hums through light and heavy cuts, and a perfect parry answers with a magnetic snap.

## v1.92 — 2026-10-21

- Recent ship, outfit and Desert updates now include pictures in See what changed.
- Several flickering joins at the Aerie and Lamp-House, and buried city roof caps, have been repaired.
- Fight-temple timers and room names make room for your hearts when using larger text in a narrow browser window.
- You can post Tansy’s letter to Hesper at the Signal Market’s night halt.
- If your browser refuses to capture the mouse, you can keep dragging to look without an unhandled error.
- Win your first hoverbike by beating Kamal in a canyon race. He rides alongside you; losses can be retried, and bikes you already earned stay yours.
- The Rose House now teaches the blade, guard and push gun in order, ending with the spark-stone that lights Qanat’s tree.
- Your first landing is a gentle powered touchdown closer to Qanat, with the city ahead of the ramp.
- The furnished porch folds into the ship before departure and opens again after touchdown.
- Wake up facing the message console, with a window onto space behind your father’s hologram and its small screen safely to one side.
- The caravan has roomier living quarters, wider doors and stairs, and finished walls around the cockpit.
- The original ship remains playable alongside the rounded caravan. Choose either ship in the dev menu’s Ship tab.
- The caravan has a curved instrument console, coral pilot seats, a framed message viewer, a compact one-metre planet hologram and more personal galley and bunk details.
- The caravan’s chairs, tea table and planters are bolted to the deck and fold into the ship together.
- Directions and conversations now name the tools you actually find, point to the uncovered ship scar and the correct signal-lamp direction, and respect expanded tanks.
- The ending remembers a visit to Hollin without claiming you made a promise or delivered news you never gave him.
- The audit dashboard now includes the completed v1.90 review, its evidence and the lowest-scoring areas to work on next.

## v1.91 — 2026-10-20

- The porch folds into the ship before departure, with its furniture secured overhead; it opens again after touchdown.
- Your ship is now the rounded caravan, with four rear boosters, a raised cockpit and furnished rooms you can walk through.
- The caravan sits closer to the ground on shorter landing legs, with a shorter boarding ramp.

## v1.90 — 2026-10-20

- Ten new outfits are available in the Wardrobe: courier, mechanic, surveyor, apprentice and festival clothes, plus five compact spacefaring suits with fitted armour and harnesses.
- The Salt Pilgrim’s robe and sash have more room at the waist, reducing clothing overlap as he runs.
- Repeated reference pictures now load reliably on the published References page.
- The caravan study has a rounder hull and broad curved cockpit. Its camp packs into a closed side hatch before lift-off, then unfolds after landing.
- Fight temples reward a clean roll with a brighter blade and a shot restored. Charged cuts and ripostes bite deeper into gorged foes, and fluid refills sooner between shots.
- The Underwater City has much more life outside its windows: dense schools of fish, nearby mantas and great whales passing above the streets and beside the Gallery.
- In Unity, drag the Wardrobe preview to turn the traveller and move the galactic scanner with a mouse or finger. See what changed opens the illustrated changelog in your browser.
- With motion matching enabled, the traveller now settles into standing after a short walk instead of holding a frozen stride with bent knees.
- The caravan study has been rebuilt around the selected drawing, with a broad cockpit, a deeper rounded hull and separate engine pods.
- The caravan study now has four rear boosters in two pairs, with a Bottom view for checking the underside.
- Unity can group more stationary scenery into shared draws without reducing its detail.

## v1.89 — 2026-10-20

- A caravan version of the traveller’s ship can be explored in a separate 3D study beside the selected drawing: a shaded porch, plants, a water tank, roof laundry and twin engines. Turn it around and compare its size with a person before the design goes into the game.
- After a short walk, the traveller keeps a balanced standing posture while looking around instead of arching backwards.
- The Underwater City’s café floors no longer shimmer where they overlap the hall paving beneath them.
- In the Wardrobe the traveller is now lit by the sun without the world’s shadows, wherever he stands, so his clothes and the sword’s colour look the same everywhere: in the Unity version he no longer comes out grey when he stands in the ship’s shadow.
- In Unity, photos and previews drawn at reduced resolution no longer have a rectangle shaded differently from the rest of the picture.
- In the Unity version, the sea over the Underwater City, seen from under the water, is the same deep, even blue as on the web instead of a pale ceiling crossed by streaks, and from the sea port’s deck the far sea is a flat tone again, without fine lines out to the horizon.
- The caravan study packs its camp before taking off: the porch folds against the hull, the awning retracts, and the plants, furniture and laundry stow away. Arriving opens the camp automatically after touchdown.
- The caravan ship study now uses the game’s ink, hatching and sunlight, so its surfaces read as they will in the world.
- In the Wardrobe, the traveller now holds the sword upright in his hand, with the whole blade visible as you browse its colours and his outfits.

## v1.88 — 2026-10-20

- In the Underwater City the ship no longer leaves you out in the sea with no way in: it now lands on a sea port, a round coral deck floating on the water over the city. A lift in the brass house in front of the ramp takes you down a glass shaft through the sea and in through the Dock’s dome, where Bastien keeps the lock: step on and stand to ride down, and ride it back up to your ship.
- In the Unity version, the graphics chip has fewer kinds of drawing to prepare before a world appears: surfaces seen from both sides no longer need their own, and on the Xbox and computers every model is laid out the same way, so a world asks for about half as many of its surfaces’ (the Desert 17 instead of 33), and the Xbox prepares 55 at the start instead of 61. On Android handhelds the models keep their own layouts, which the Retroid draws faster, so its frame rate is as before. The worlds look the same.
- The game menu has a new last page, the Wardrobe, one LB / L1 from the Quests: the traveller stands on the right, drawn and lit as in the world, breathing and glancing about and turning slowly (turn him yourself with the right stick or by dragging), and on the left you choose your sword’s colour among those you have won in the fight temples, shown on a sword standing beside him as you move through them. A / × wears it at once; B / ○ leaves him as he was. His outfits will be chosen there too once the new traveller arrives.
- In the Unity version, the menus and a minigame’s cards now take the mouse and the touch screen, as on the web: point at a line or a tile to pick it, click or tap to choose, click a tab, a side arrow, a close or Back button, drag a slider, scroll a list with the wheel or a finger, and move and zoom the map with the mouse. After pausing a minigame with its button, Resume and Retry work by a tap. On a handheld, tapping the screen keeps the prompts in its buttons’ names instead of turning them into keyboard keys, and a paused minigame keeps its controls and its missiles line under the card, as on the web.
- In the Unity version, the minigames have their pause button at the top right, as on the web: click it with the mouse or tap it to pause, as Menu does. It goes away while you play with a controller and comes back as soon as you use the mouse or the screen.
- In the Unity version, the sun’s sharpest shadows now follow you as on the web, not the camera: Home’s stepping stones cast their little shadows at dusk again, the Overnight Train’s platform wall throws a smooth shadow of the right length, and the glowing screen on the Signal Market’s roof console no longer drops a dark patch down the console when you are across the roof from it.
- In the Unity version, the sun’s shadows are filtered as on the web, their edges softer up close, and the near ones sit as tight against what casts them as on the web.
- In the Unity version, a makers’ box’s card names what you found as the web writes it, with its little star before it, and its buttons are no longer in capitals; in the chime pirates, the controls and the missiles-and-gun line sit in two boxes, as on the web.
- In the Unity version, Plumb’s trail and the hoverbike’s and the skiff’s trails are drawn in their soft bands of colour (in the fluid’s colours when the backpack powers them), their tails breaking up into dots, as on the web, instead of a plain white streak.
- Walking in a fountain’s basin there, your feet no longer sink into its floor.
- In the Signal Market and on the City-Shaft’s rim, the statues by the fragments are carved figures now: a quiet one with its broad head, holding a light, listening or lifting its little screen, and a lamplighter in his long coat and wide hat with his lamp. No two fountains, bandstands, water tanks, stall blocks, signal platforms or kiosks in one world look the same any more.
- In the Desert, Marrow’s red rag is now a broad red cloth on a taller mast, with a long red pennant streaming from its top: from the tree’s roots you can make it out in the arch of Qanat’s gate, against the sky.
- At the end of the Whale-House, the last picture now shows the way out whole, from beside its door, instead of half hidden behind the Listener’s shell.
- Each makers’ house now ends on its own picture of what calming its keeper changed, after the keeper and its chimes: the Rose House’s cistern fills, the Clock-House’s stopped wall clocks race round to the right time, the tide spreads over the Whale-House’s floor, the Greenhouse’s beds fill with water, the Lamp-House’s lamp lights, and so on, instead of every house ending on the makers’ mark over a door. Then the way out opens, seen from inside its own hall. In the Whale-House the chimes land beside you and are in view, no longer under the Listener’s shell.
- The traveller is rebuilt: his body and each piece of his clothing are now made apart, ready for him to change outfits. He looks as he did, his shirt still flies out behind him when he runs, and far off he is lighter to draw.

## v1.87 — 2026-10-19

- Five guardians no longer hit you from past their arms: the Gardener’s and the Listener’s sweeps, the Clockwork Foreman’s hammers, jabs and spin, the Tooth-Warden’s charge and the Last Founder’s ladle and slam reached up to 2.7 m beyond their bodies, and now land only where the body itself passes, as every other guardian’s blows do. Stand clear of the arm and it misses you. The Foreman’s hammers still reach a guard held in front of it, on a handheld as on a computer.
- In the Signal Market and out on the City-Shaft’s rim, the fragments of the singing light no longer wait on little plinths at the foot of tower walls. Most now sit on top of something you climb to: a water tank on stilts, a stall block, a signal platform or a kiosk in the market, a cistern, a cab-wrights’ shed, a lamplighters’ platform or a pavilion on the rim, each with a stair or a stack of crates up to its roof. The others rest in the middle of a little square, on a fountain, at a statue’s feet or in a bandstand. Those you already found stay found.
- A dozen sounds that stood a little over the music since it was raised now sit just under it: the third, heavy swing of the blade and its blow landing, the double jump, the heartbeat at low health, a perfect dodge’s slow-down, the gun’s shot, the fan, a bomb, the boomerang’s aim and its catch, a swimming stroke, and the chime of a quest’s step or a cleared room. The music is as loud as before.
- In the Hush-House’s fight court in Lorn, the dark frames on the two crates no longer flicker and stripe over their lids as you turn the camera.
- In the Desert, the red rag over Marrow’s hollow now flies from a tall mast, which you see through Qanat’s main gate from the tree’s roots. In the Glass Dunes, the carriers have left their oil jars where the way up to the breaker’s cairn passes nearest the half-buried court, and a row of their posts leads you there.
- In the Mooring-House each lock now shows its answer from where you stand: the Chain Locker is the room behind the west door, its ring and its ball in sight of the far door they open; the moorers’ gate has two keyholes, one for the Rope Store’s key and one for a key in the Anchor Hold, whose cage opens when you pull the spare anchor home and wake its eye; and from the great door you see the lamp-eye it wants through a window beside the shaft’s gate. In the Footprint, the Lens Chamber’s door takes a small key, waiting on the Still Pool’s far ledge.
- From Lorn on, the fight temples now count the heart sold at the Almonry in Vael and the one at the Welcome-Shelf in Lorn among the hearts you can have on arriving, so their blows strike as hard as they were tuned to for a traveller who bought every heart: about an eighth harder than before.
- A makers’ chest behind a court’s gate or a small lock is no longer pointed out before you have the tool that opens it. The temple’s keeper gives you its quest, the locals tell you where it is and the pale column rises over its court only once that tool is yours, most often after the same world’s makers’ house.
- Every fragment of the singing light now sits on something instead of a bare ledge or corner: inside a ribcage, on a roof or a temple’s steps, a tower’s balcony, the high ring round the City-Shaft’s spire, a moon’s courtyard, never more than two on one place and spread over the whole world; where a world had no landmark left, a little stone cairn or plinth holds one. Most of them moved; the first one in the Desert sings at the cairn beside your ship, and people’s hints name the place. Those you already found stay found.
- The first time you pick up a fragment of the singing light, Anselm no longer just appears beside you: you see him run in from out of sight, his brass box ticking faster as he comes, and stop beside you out of breath before he speaks. B / ○ skips it.
- The chime pirates’ flights now take you through places of their own. The Rose Rings: a rose giant ringed with rubble. The Iron Moon: you skim a heavy moon whose pull drags your ship down. The Frost Moon: ice spires rise from its face into your way. The Storm Giant: over its clouds, pirates hide in the storms until they are close, only their lamps showing, with lightning in the clouds. Each flight opens as your ship drops out of the warp, with the place’s name, and each place has its own light, sky and sound; played from the Arcade, Fly on takes you to the next one.
- In the Unity version on the Retroid, big buildings, roots and carvings far away are drawn with fewer triangles where the difference is smaller than half a pixel, so Lorn’s start now runs at 60 frames a second too, with the picture unchanged: 23 of the 25 measured views hold 60.
- When a conversation ends, however it ends, the traveller lets go of his talking pose at once, instead of sometimes staying stuck in it, leaning back with a hand on his hip, after you walk off.
- Marrow by the ship, and the people who wear a strap across the chest in the City-Shaft, Viridel, the Garden of Spheres, Lorn and the Signal Market, now wear it lying on them, over the shoulder and round to the hip, instead of a straight stick from the shoulder down to where the other hand hangs. Marrow’s bag sits on his hip.
- Arriving in the desert is smooth again: for about seven seconds after it loaded, the game ran at 30 to 45 frames a second while it worked out the ground under the salt lagoons and the gorge’s stream for the water’s colours. That now takes under three seconds, a little each frame, and the game stays at full speed meanwhile, on handhelds too.
- In the desert, water lies in its bed instead of floating over the sand: the channels by Qanat’s new fields and the one from the Rose House, the flooded salt pan, the edges of the salt lagoons and the gorge’s stream, and the water risen in the qanat wells.
- Once the Rose House’s keeper is calm, its water runs down the middle of the house’s steps, falling over each one to the sand, instead of sticking out of the door into the air and vanishing under the stairs.
- In the Unity version on the Retroid and other small Android handhelds, the picture drops to a slightly softer resolution where the GPU can’t keep up and comes back where it can, so 22 of the 25 measured views now run at 60 frames a second instead of 10 (the City-Shaft looking across, Lorn’s start and the Floating city’s start are still under it).
- When Plumb talks to you, its “This way.” and “Look.” are no longer stuck in the dialogue box pointing at nothing: it says them at the top of the screen as it flies off. At a Blade Gate it warns you in a whisper and stays with you instead of flying off.
- Plumb now talks to you: when it has something important to say (you seem lost, a makers’ house has you stuck, a gate means danger), it comes to your shoulder with a red mark and a chirp instead of a line at the top of the screen. Press its button (D-pad → or Q) to hear it in the dialogue box, then it flies off to show you the way.
- In the Unity version, the game menu’s sheet, tabs and strip sit where the web’s do, so the Map is as large as on the web and its ground in the same place, and someone with a quest for you is marked as on the web. On the web, photo mode no longer shows the gadget in your hand in the corner of your pictures.
- In the Unity version, the Quests page lists the quests you finished close together under Done and shows each quest’s goal on its card, Items fits as many to a row as the web, and the Map says how many fragments you found there.
- In the Unity version, What’s new opens over the game in its own panel and B closes it, and the Start menu shows your fragments on a line of their own, the version beside Settings and CONTROLS over “Your keys”, as on the web.
- In the Unity version, Anselm’s shop opens over a darkened hold with its names and prices in heavy letters and every flag inside its box, as on the web, and a conversation’s box is as tall from its first page as when its answers come up.
- Playing in a phone’s browser, the game no longer reloads itself every few seconds. It now needs about a third less of the phone’s memory there, under what Safari lets a page hold before it reloads it; an iPhone gets the Handheld graphics, as Android phones do; and a swipe down on the title or in a menu no longer pulls the page down to reload it.
- Small fixes: Joss now says the machine letting the Mooring-House’s cables out is behind a barred gate, not at the top; the Quests page says Plumb finds your quest; a house’s map chest names your own button for the game menu; with Reduce motion on, a fight temple’s camera no longer slides toward a hazard; the first time R3 turns the camera behind you, a line says so; Nima’s dust joke sounds like a joke; Fenna’s gallery is the old city’s everywhere; Anselm’s box ticks, and he tells “some” singing from “a little”.
- At your parents’ stone, Anselm’s lamp now lights the round window after the last words are said, without counting the splinters. The tune he sells stops short of its end, since only Ilen can give the light’s song back whole.
- The twelve fight temples’ hosts each speak in their own voice now, each with a reason of their own and a last word of their own, instead of all promising to get back to work once you clear the place. Three people were renamed so no two names you meet blur together: the cistern’s digger is Omar, the Seed Vault’s keeper Abner and the Kiln Yard’s Mirek, in colours of his own. A fight temple’s cards count the foes you sent back into the floor and show your own buttons for aiming and rolling.
- In the Unity version, the chime pirates’ place names and a minigame’s other call-outs, the chimes you carry and the numbers on the gadget marks are set in the game’s own lettering, as on the web, instead of a typewriter face.

## v1.86 — 2026-10-19

- In the Glass Dunes a row of float-posts now leads west from the landing, through the red cleft and down the folded ravine to the pearl city, and home by the gap. Fenna, by the cleft’s arch, asks you to read the old city’s clock from its high gallery.
- The city under the Glass Dunes’ sand no longer shimmers as you walk its lanes: the rooms jutting from its houses, their ledges and windows no longer lie in one another’s faces, and the same goes for the stairs and bridges where they meet a terrace, the pale floors over the sand and the pearl city’s tower windows.
- Knocked off a high ledge, your limp body now falls the way it was going instead of drifting sideways as it tumbles, so it no longer swings back under the ledge it fell from and lands on the ground below as it should.
- The films that end each makers’ house no longer look through the guardian. The shot of what it leaves you shows the chimes on the floor with the guardian beyond them, where it used to be filmed from inside its leaves, its moss or a wall; the chimes now land in the open in front of it rather than under it. The Lampless’s wings and the Listener’s shell no longer fill the first shot, the Gardener is no longer washed pale by its sunbeam in the wide over its hall, and after the Whale-House’s film the camera comes back beside you rather than under the Listener’s shell.
- On screens faster than 60 frames a second, and in the Unity version, your first steps out of the ship at the end of the opening no longer stop short at the foot of the ramp with the camera caught in its railing: the ship walks you out to your first view of the desert, as at 60.
- The soft-fall soles, the wind-silk scarf and the brass level are no longer on top of their climbs: they wait in the makers’ courts of the City-Shaft, Vael’s sky stones and the Glass Dunes.
- The little chests on high places now have a small lock beside them that one of your tools opens: braziers to light with ember, rings to tug with the hook, jaws to still, a bud to bloom, a plate to stomp, a cracked cap to blast, an iron disc for the magnet glove, a ring for the tether, a lamp to float up to, and one chest only the seeing lens can show.
- Every makers’ court’s chest now waits behind a gate its puzzle opens, and every court’s puzzle uses a tool you already have when you reach that world. Four courts that had no chest now have one, among them a new charm in the Signal Market that lets the recall hourglass reach further.
- The ending now counts the fragments: Ilen answers in more steps for how many you brought, Anselm’s lamp goes in the round window at the true ending if you bought it, and the credits say how many splinters of her song you carried home.
- Anselm has more to sell once his upgrades are yours: his notebook, a page at a time, a lamp for the hold that grows brighter with every fragment, the light’s whole tune, and, once you have found a hundred, his chart: your Map then marks every fragment still hiding in the world you are in.
- The game no longer tells you how many fragments of the singing light there are. Picking one up, the Worlds page, the Start menu and Anselm now say how many you have found, and Anselm tells you roughly how much still sings in the world you are in.
- In the fight temples, the hazards that lie on the floor take far fewer hearts: the Flooded Gallery’s dark is shorter, no quicker in the last wave and its lamps light a wider circle; burning oil, breaking tiles and slag give you a moment to step off before they burn, and the oil opens nearer you, where you can see it. The toll, the millstones, the ore carts and the falling loads hurt a little less, and no hazard grows stronger in the later worlds.
- The fight temples now cost about the same share of your hearts from the first to the last, instead of the first costing the most. Each one’s blows and hazards are weighed against the hearts you can have on reaching its world: the Sunken Hall, with your first three hearts, no longer takes more than all of them, and the last ones hit harder for the hearts you bring. A small blow is no longer rounded up to a quarter heart, so the light foes really strike lightly, one blow never takes more than a heart, and the rank weighs the hearts you lost against the hearts you came with.
- The Start menu (Menu) is shorter: Items, Quests and Map are in the game menu (View) alone, where they already were. Inside a makers’ house, its map is now a tab of that menu too, after Map, and ▲ ▼ go up and down its floors.
- In the Unity version, the Controls page shows your keys and buttons on caps, two to a line, as on the web; the thing in your hand carries its red IN USE tag in Items; and photo mode’s line of keys reads as the web’s.
- A new controller layout. Hold D-pad ↑ for the wheel: every gadget and each of the glove’s modes now has its own slot, and the game waits while you choose, so no foe hits you meanwhile. A tap of D-pad ↑ takes back your last pick, so push and fluid are a tap apart. D-pad → now calls Plumb to find your objective (or a hint in a guardian’s fight), riding too, and R3 only locks on: with no foe near, it turns the camera behind you.
- In the Mooring-House you now see the Anchor-Warden from the first hall, through the bars of its gate, and when it is stopped the gate opens and you walk back out into the hall where you began. Its floor is reached by a great door off the Cord Loft. Where it stood, past the west door, the Anchor Hold keeps a spare anchor out over a well: pull it home with the tether to draw back the moorers’ gate’s bar.
- In the Builders’ Greenhouse the bloom is found first, on a mound in the middle of the great glass hall, and the whole house is about it from there: the Cold Frame’s door is a flower-door in the sun, its trays hold seeds to bring the sun to, and the Seed Chamber’s seed wants the water sent up before it grows and opens the way on.
- The Lamp-House runs in a new order. The Kennel’s door now wakes to a glowing orb you roll across the first hall, so its keepers and their small key come before the bubble wand. With the wand, the great key in the Wick Room is yours half-way through, in the dark, and the great lamp is lit on the way back.
- Pressing lock-on (R3 / Tab) in a fight temple now answers: the aim’s ring flashes under the foe your blade will take, with the lock’s tick, and the first time a line says the right stick or the mouse aims in there.
- The sign on each fight temple room’s floor is now the makers’ mark in a faint square. It was two dark rings, the very shape of the foes’ ring attacks, under every fight.
- In the fight temples a hazard that starts near the edge of the screen (a pool of oil, a falling load, a breaking tile, an ink geyser) now draws the view toward it so you see it coming, and one still out of sight gets a marker at the screen’s edge, as a foe winding up out of sight does.
- In the fight temples you no longer lose yourself in the crowd: when foes cover you or close in round you, an inked ring with a gold mark over your head shows where you are.
- Plumb now has a word the first time you come near a fight temple’s gate, Sabri in the desert and Teasel in Lorn mention the ink under the old floors, and Items names each colour after the place you won it, as in Ember · the Sunken Hall.
- In the Unity version, the City-Shaft’s banners keep their pale colours and glyphs when they wave, where they turned into dark slabs, and the makers’ towers, the Rose House’s red one first, show their cut stone blocks as on the web.
- In the Unity version, the Rose House’s lit circuits glow orange up its walls as on the web, instead of thin brown lines: a line of light two pixels wide no longer has the pen’s outline drawn over it, here or anywhere else.
- People now tell you of a fight temple as the old floor the ink comes up through and the person waiting by its door, not as a game, and your map names it by the place.
- The fight temples now belong to the story: the makers built them over springs where the dark wells up, and the people by their doors want their mill, their cranes or their gardens back, and say so. A run ends with the place going quiet and its keeper coming back in, before your time and rank, and the ember shard’s double cuts are now the blade running bright.

## v1.85 — 2026-10-18

- On Gentle, the fight temples’ hazards that take a quarter heart (a gust, the eyes’ flash, the toll, the dark, the shade, a broken tile, burning oil) now take half as much over a fight. They were meant to take half as much on Gentle, but half of a quarter heart came back as a whole one.
- In the Kiln Yard, the Glass Dunes’ fight temple, a tile about to break now shows it: its edge glows and a dark star of cracks spreads across it. The thin white cracks it had were all but invisible on the pale glass, and the breaking tiles took more hearts than anything else in there.
- In the Mooring-House, the great key’s last ring hangs on the house’s great cable: while the moored room is up, it is hauled out of reach under the roof, and you have to send the room down without you to bring it back within reach. The cables and chains in the house now hang from the roof, instead of stopping in mid-air.
- In the Footprint, the seeing lens now shows you where to look. Raise it as you leave the Lens Chamber: the walker’s prints lead round the balcony to the foot of the hidden spiral, and it shows as soon as you look at it from there. The walker’s print is on the wall again by the chasm of hidden stones, and the first drop opens just outside the Hall of Spheres’ door, where you can see it from the puzzle that opens it.
- In the Unity version too, the loading screen’s bar fills as the world is prepared, with the small line under it saying what is taking the time.
- In Vael, the makers’ box with the wind-silk scarf is no longer lost under the cloud, where it fell when its stack of balanced stones was moved. It now sits on top of Tiv’s stack on the great table’s west rim, in sight of the riders’ halfway stone.
- In Lorn, the way to the rebuilt Hush-House is easy to find again: a lit crystal now crowns its tower, seen from the Great Crystal across the swamp, and the keepers’ stakes run from the crystal cave’s south mouth along the house’s shore to its steps, not out over the open water.
- People no longer start in the middle of a plant: in Viridel Mira stood inside a ribbon plant, in the desert Marrow and in Lorn Tansy had a flower growing right where they stood. Plants now keep off the spots people start from and the paths they walk.
- The flowers, fans and fungi that answer you no longer grow on roofs: in Lorn they stood on the Hush-House’s round roof, in the Garden of Spheres on the pyramid’s top, in Viridel on a stall, and on buildings in the Glass Dunes. They grow on the ground now, and the plants of the Sky Stones no longer stand on the slabs and sand banks of Vael’s plain.
- The Moon Foundry’s and the City Floating in Space’s big moments show what happens again. Out of the Casting-House, the film no longer opens on two seconds of a dark wall: it starts wide on the house and its tower from out on the floor. Over the Mooring-House, the cables drawing taut are filmed from under the great tripod, between its legs, instead of through one of them, and the first shot no longer presses against the house’s wall.
- Each world’s recorded music plays louder, about twice as loud as before: it had been sitting well under the footsteps, the blade and the voices, so a fight or a conversation drowned it. Now the music holds its own under them, as the sounds were meant to sit, and nothing in it distorts.
- A house’s great key now looks the part: its bow is the makers’ mark in brass and pale stone, a soft teal light runs down its shaft and its ward steps like a little staircase. Finding it, or the house’s map, now brings up a card as a makers’ box does, the key held over its chest until you go on.
- The hearts and the key count no longer show over a makers’ box as it opens, as they did in the City-Shaft’s Ash Store after its fight: every box’s scene, like every other scene, keeps the screen clear whatever your health.
- In the Unity version, a conversation’s answers are marked as on the web: with a controller, the A button on the answer you are on and a small arrow by the others, instead of the keyboard’s numbers.
- Switching the glove’s modes, each one now has a sound of its own: a wet rise for fluid, a low whump for push, a soft rising pair for bloom and a taut twang for tether, besides stilling’s high chirp and ember’s warm one. The stilling shot, the hook’s reel, the magnet glove and the spring boots no longer play the very same sound every time, and the push, the bombs and the boots’ stomp are less deafening, the lens and the boots’ wound-up ping quieter, and the boots’ winding easier to hear.
- In the Garden of Spheres, the grass tufts no longer flicker as you turn the camera. They were drawn in black ink and read as dark clumps, and their thin outlines came and went with every small move; now they are green, outlined in a darker green, and the Garden’s small flowers take a lighter line from afar as Viridel’s do.
- In the chime pirates you can now boost (RB / R1) and brake (LB / L1) to meet the rocks and mines sooner or later, on a gauge that fills again. Gunboats, bombers and whole waves of fighters leave gold drops: fly through them for rapid fire, a spread shot and then the storm guns, until a hit takes you back to the twin bolts. The roll is on B / ○ and X / □.
- The chime pirates’ flights now differ from one world to the next: a minefield strung across the way, an ice belt drifting across it and a field of old wrecks, each with fighters in formations of its own and a sky of its own. Your ship is drawn smaller, so there is room to dodge round it; it no longer shimmers as the fight starts, and the fight sounds shorter and cleaner instead of ringing on.
- People in capes coming into sight no longer make the game catch for a moment, most of all on the Retroid: a cape now settles over a few frames instead of all in one. Walking through the camps and the City-Shaft, the frames over 50 ms on the Retroid fell from 12 to 4.
- Shadows keep up with whatever casts them again on the Handheld, Low, Steam Deck and Xbox settings, and in the Unity version on the Steam Deck and handhelds. The shadows close to you were redrawn only every second or fourth frame there, so yours and the foes’ trailed a step behind and moved in jerks. Only the shadows of the far scenery, which barely moves, are still redrawn less often.
- The loading screen now tells you what it is doing and why it takes the time. Under the turning pen a bar fills as each part is done, and a small line says what that part is: dressing the traveller, the walls learning where you can’t walk, teaching the graphics card the world’s light (slowest the first time).
- The fight temples load faster. While loading, each one got every kind of foe in the game ready, though only four or five come up in it; now it readies just those, so the graphics card has a third fewer shaders to learn.
- In the Unity version, choosing Continue or a save on the title now shows the loading screen, the pen turning and what is being prepared, instead of the title freezing until the world appears.
- In the Unity version, the makers’ mark in a conversation or a speech balloon is the game’s own open-corner mark, as on the web, not a different sign.
- In the Unity version, the menus show the controller’s buttons as the web does: a small button inside the one it presses (A in the entry you are on, B in Resume and Back, LB and RB on the side tabs), and the prompts at the foot as buttons too, instead of their names written out. On the Retroid they carry its own names.
- Out in the open, the wings carry you about a quarter further on still air. Inside the makers’ houses they glide as before.
- The gun’s push sends the makers’ balls much further: about half the pushes for a long roll. Push from further back for a gentle nudge onto a nearby plate.
- In Vael, the rider’s call you play on the bone flute to bring the bird down is quieter: it now sits with the world’s music instead of ringing out over it.
- The fireflies drifting in the dark of Lorn’s Lamp-House are fewer, smaller and softer: little sparks again, not a hall of floating lamps.
- Creatures and machines now look at you once they have noticed you: the crab’s eye stalks lean your way, the toad’s eyes turn in their sockets, the lizard, the hound and the heron turn their heads, the wisp’s eyes slide over its petals and the brute’s brass eye follows you round as its body twists to you. Left alone, they look where they are going.
- The Unity version is a smaller download: the Android app is 111 MB instead of 130, and the Steam Deck’s download 186 MB instead of 429. The game’s shaders were packed twice, the second copy never read, and the Deck’s archive carried the build’s working files, which it never installed.
- On the Quests page, the quest you are tracking now stands out, framed in red with a TRACKING tag, and the quest you are on shows the button to track it instead (A / × on a controller, Enter on the keyboard).
- In the games’ menus (the start card, the pause, the results), the button you are on is now the one drawn in colour, filled red as in the other menus. Retry, Start or Resume no longer look picked when they aren’t: a red edge at their left says which one Enter presses.
- Lorn’s skiff is solid end to end: its bow, its stern and the float off its side stop at the small crystals rising from the water instead of sailing through them. Its sail is set out over the side now, so it no longer runs through your face as you ride.
- In the Unity version, D-pad ↓ calls your mount again: in Lorn the skiff comes over to you, as it always did on the web.
- The hearts no longer stay up over a world’s filmed moment because a foe was chasing you as it began.
- In the Unity version, the chimes a felled foe leaves lie on a soft violet patch of shade, as on the web, not on a white disc.
- In the Unity version, flowers far across Viridel’s meadow are drawn in the light line they have on the web, not as black blots of ink; the cables and vines under the City in Space’s platforms stay whole lines far off instead of breaking into dashes; and the Whale-House’s great shell has its spiral and the seams of its chambers.
- In the Unity version, the first room of every makers’ house is as light as on the web: the glow of the way you came in by brightens it as it does there, where before the whole room came out grey and dim. And Lorn’s lake has its pale shallows along the shores again, the water darkening as it deepens.
- On the Steam Deck, Hiraeth is now one game in your library and always starts the Unity version. It no longer slips into the older web version when the Unity one is slow to start or not downloaded yet: a first start downloads it with a progress bar on the screen, and if it truly cannot start, a message says so and you are back in your library. The Hiraeth (web) shortcut leaves your library the next time the game updates while Steam is closed.
- Foes arriving no longer freeze the game for a moment, most of all on the Retroid and in the Unity version: every foe of a kind now shares one body shape instead of building its own, and the foes a world can field are prepared while it loads. When a brute and a swarm of skitters come in, the longest frame on the Retroid is about half what it was.
- Opening the Map for the first time in a world no longer freezes the game for a moment: its ground is drawn a little each frame while the page says how far along it is. In the Unity version the longest frame of a trip through the menus and the Map went from about 190 ms to 29.
- Riding the bird in Vael, her wings no longer shimmer as they beat. Their feathers took turns in two tones, and seen from the saddle each wing was a bundle of thin lines crawling with every stroke; now each row of feathers is one tone.
- In the Rose House, the watcher’s aiming beam now stops at your shield when you hold it up, and at you when you don’t, instead of running on through you and out the other side.
- The fight temples’ end card no longer says your sword and shield wear the new colours before you have them: they take them once found. And people no longer tell of a game you can’t play yet, a trial that wants the hoverbike or a gadget you haven’t found.
- In Lorn, locking on to a bellows toad near a bank or a rise in the ground no longer freezes the game. As the toad looked for high ground to spit from, its search broke and the game stopped.
- Edda at the night halt, Nima and Ossa in the City-Shaft get to the point: the letter or the splinter comes within four or five pages instead of nine to twelve, and the rest is there if you ask. Madame Sel and Wen are a little shorter too, and the Signal Market’s first step now says whom you are looking for: “Hear Madame Sel out by the landing”.
- The Clock-House’s and the Undertower’s last steps now point the right way: their guardians wait upstairs, and the steps used to say go down.
- Each world’s own recorded music now plays the whole time you are there, out in the open as well as on arriving, indoors and at the big moments. If you liked it better the old way, with the music for the moments and quieter, lighter tunes in between, choose Moments under Settings > Music plays.

## v1.84 — 2026-10-17

- In the Unity version, the traveller’s eyes and brows are as sharp as on the web: close up and in conversations they were soft, blurred patches, and now they show the white, the iris, the pupil and its glint and the fine lash lines. People’s eyes are drawn as on the web too: a crisp ring round the iris, a clear glint, the lids open at rest and a lash line along them.
- Each world’s own recorded music now plays the first time you land there, on the Xbox and in the Android app too, where it is downloaded the first time it is needed: it comes in as soon as it has arrived, faded in. Before, the Unity version played the game’s synthesised score for your whole first visit to every world but the Desert, and the Android app could wait minutes for the next moment to bring it in.
- In the Unity version, the traveller’s face moves as on the web: he blinks, smiles, raises his brows and opens his mouth as he talks (it stayed still). The ship’s rooms keep their shading strokes clear instead of a grey blur; plants lean away from you along the path you walk through them; the camera pressed into the traveller leaves him out of the view; a struck foe’s lines flash white; and the shading round foes by a wall no longer darkens round them.
- In the Unity version on the Steam Deck, the first launch after an update no longer freezes for three and a half seconds just after a world with foes appears: what the game needs to draw the foes, the water from below, the waterfalls, the shade under a jump and the marks on the ground is now made ready while the world loads (that one loading takes 2–3 seconds longer).
- In the Unity version, the title’s name sits centred across the top on every screen, as on the web: on the Retroid’s 4:3 screen it opened pushed aside the first time, and a window resized or a screen turned kept the old layout.
- In the Unity version, the marks lying on the desert’s ground are drawn whole, as on the web: the sand river’s pale ribbon, the tide lines along the dry wash, the salt pan, the basin’s wet shore, the sand banked against things and the crash’s scrapes no longer break into jagged pieces with the ground showing through.
- In the Unity version the water looks as on the web from below and in the sun: under water the view sinks into the water’s colour in flat bands, and in the Underwater City pale shafts of light come down from the sea’s surface and caustics play on the floors, behind the halls’ glass too; above water the sun glints in white dashes on the water toward it (not on the Handheld, Steam Deck and Xbox presets, as on the web). Sounds are muffled under water there too, and the camera no longer stops half in, half out of the water.
- In the Unity version the waterfalls fall as on the web: the great curtain over the City Behind the Waterfall and its small deep fall stream down in bands of teal, turquoise and white with pen streaks, glassy at the lip and misty at the foot, with slits you can see through; the Buried Machine’s oil fall, Viridel’s stair fall and the Glass Dunes’ sand falls too. They were flat sheets.
- In the Unity version, “ride the bird” shows over the bird when you stand by it, as on the web; a scene’s black bars slide in as slowly and smoothly as on the web’s; and the fluid’s splats lie whole on the ground the moment they land (the ground hid them, in part or all, a beat longer).
- Arriving in the City-Shaft, the game’s check that no far-off tree stands inside a house takes about half the time, with the same trees in the end: on the Steam Deck it is done half a minute after you arrive instead of nearly a minute, and the frames it slowed are back to full speed that much sooner.
- The game’s own work each frame is lighter on slow processors: the Underwater City’s rising bubbles are only worked out where you can see them, and the posing of the traveller and the people walks their skeletons faster. In the Unity version on the Steam Deck that is up to 7 more frames a second in the Underwater City and up to 2 more in the other worlds.
- In the Unity version, the worlds that have no drifting cloud shadows on the web (Lorn, Viridel, the Garden, the Signal Market and most others) have none in Unity either: Lorn’s marsh was all in shade, and Viridel’s far white tower under a cloud.
- In the Unity version, more of the world moves and shines as on the web: the Garden’s still lake shows the far shore and the garden it remembers upside down in it, the Signal Market’s billboards turn into lit screens after dark, the Buried Machine’s great pistons slide out of the canyon walls and its vents puff steam, Lorn’s crystal veins glow under the water, the fluid’s splats pop out and shrink away instead of standing whole, and the grass parts along the last steps you took, not only round your feet.
- In the Unity version, each frame spends less time copying the world over to Unity: only what changed is looked at again, not every building and rock. Up to 3 more frames a second in the City-Shaft and the Signal Market. And a lamp’s brief pulse in the Underwater City, which Unity used to miss, now shows.
- In the Unity version, a game started from the Arcade offers the game before and after and the way back to the Arcade on its cards, and a fight temple’s start card shows its hall from above, as on the web.
- In the Unity version, the true ending’s credits roll up the screen on their paper page, the worlds and the people you met, as on the web, instead of being read out a line at a time.
- In the Unity version, Settings is laid out as on the web, in its groups (Screen and text, Camera and motion, Controls, Sound, Game) and order, and it has the web’s alien voices, run and guard, enemies and drone hints settings too.
- In the Unity version, the shops, the Arcade’s board and a game’s cards look like the web’s: the keeper’s line in a speech bubble and the wares as cards with their pictures and prices, the games as inked cards with their coloured dots, and the cards in the web’s typeface with their rules in a dashed box.
- In the browser and the Android app, each frame takes less work to get ready to draw on a slow processor: the world is looked over once a frame to find what is in view, instead of once for every picture the frame is made of (its shadows and the view itself). The picture is exactly the same.
- In the Unity version, the lamps of the City During the Eclipse, the makers’ houses and the ship’s rooms pool their own warm colour on the floor round them, as on the web, instead of a white glare.
- In the Unity version of the fight temples, a wall or pillar between you and the camera opens a ragged hole round you, as on the web, so a fight is never hidden behind it.
- In the Unity version, your sword’s blade is living water again: turquoise with cream currents and rising bubbles, a bright wavering edge, bowing back through a swing, a ring of light running along it when it hits; the guard’s shield too. Before, it was a thin grey blade.
- In the Unity version, the foes wear the patterns painted on them as on the web: the crabs’ star spots and mottling, the lizards’ scales and banded bellies, the centipede’s rivets and rust, the moths’ glowing lanterns, and the brute’s glowing cracks, which open wider as you hurt it. Before, each was its plain colours.
- In the Unity version, the shafts of light in the skull cave and the makers’ houses are as soft and pale as on the web, no longer bright white slabs.
- In the Unity version, the dark of the makers’ houses’ deadly pits no longer shows through walls, and the shafts of light, the doorways’ glow, the floating motes and the recordings’ figures are hidden behind what stands in front of them again: drawn straight to the screen, they had read the scene’s depth upside down.
- In the Unity version, the world dims behind the “You were knocked out” card as it does on the web, and the card sits high with its gold Restart button.
- In the Unity version, the menus look like the web’s: the pause screen’s yellow side with PAUSED in big lettering and its buttons in thick ink, the settings on ruled paper with their sliders, switches and boxes, the game menu’s tabs, quest cards and strip on a warm desk, and every menu, hint and conversation in the web’s own typeface instead of a typewriter’s.
- In the Unity version, the use button over a person or a thing is the round button alone, as on the web, instead of a dark strip, and a hint that names a button shows the button as a badge before its words.
- In the Unity version, a world loads behind the same inked page as on the web, the pen turning in its rings and a line saying what is being drawn, where the screen used to stay black.
- In the Unity version, a new game no longer hands you the desert’s hoverbike as the opening ends. It lies under Marrow’s tarp in the hollow by the red rag again, and finding it starts its errand, as on the web. A save that was given it that way gets it back under the tarp.
- The City Floating in Space’s sky-boats are longer and rounder, as on their design: a nine-metre capsule with its top and its belly in two colours, a long low cabin with lit windows, portholes down each side, two lamps in the nose and a fin and a screw at the tail. The boats moored in the Pier’s hoops sit inside them.
- The City Behind the Waterfall’s landing is a proper port now, closer to its design: a docking niche is cut into the cliff ahead of your ship, its arch opening on a lit hall with a loading dock and steps up to it, a gantry bolted over it with a crate on its cable, crates on the dock and at its foot, puddles and moss on the wet stone, and a second courier parked beside the first. Tully waits by the niche.
- In the Unity version, the hearts and the potion look as on the web: a part-filled heart fills by its quarters with thin lines between them (it filled round like a clock), its glint and shadow as on the web, the last heart pulsing when you are low; the potion beside them is the corked flask with its red draught (it was a red blob), its count in the web’s heavy figures, faded when you have none.
- In the Unity version, a patch of shade lies under you whenever you jump, fall or glide, so you can judge where you will land, as on the web. The traveller’s captured jumps, idles, starts and stops and the people’s own walks play there too, and “Back to the Arcade” puts you in front of the game you just played.
- In the Unity version, the fluid gun shows what it does as on the web: aiming puts the inked crosshair in the middle of the screen with the mode’s name under it, red and turned on what it would lock, faint when the tank is empty, kicking on a hit; a mode switch shows its name beside you a moment, and a tank run dry says “empty” there. The magic bar has its ticks between units and turns pale and hatched when there is not enough left for a shot.
- In the Unity version, fights answer as on the web: fire and spines burn at the web’s rate (they bit twice as fast), a blow lands with a dull thud under the cry, the hearts stay up for the whole fight, the chimes count up into the wallet, the fight temples and the games shake the camera on a hit, the chime-pirates meet the ship on its first flight to a new world, and in the Lab R3 and L3 hop between the worlds’ rooms.
- On the City-Shaft’s rim your ship now sets down into a lacquered cradle hung between two tall towers: the cradle, swung out toward the shaft, swings it in over the rim’s paving, rocks past its place and settles before the ramp opens. When you leave, it swings the ship out again and it lifts off from the cradle.
- In the Underwater City your ship now comes down the Dock’s lock: it sets down on the lift’s coral plate high between four teal columns banded in brass, and the lock lowers it onto the dome before the ramp opens. When you leave, the lock lifts it up again and it takes off from there.
- Each port has someone who runs it: Orla keeps the Pier’s harbour book, Faro wardens the City-Shaft’s rim docks, Ludo logs the Signal Market’s call-signs, Mabyn keeps the Float Yard’s tally, Cato walks ships in on the Eclipse city’s esplanade, Tully keeps the Waterfall’s ledge and Dace loads the Buried Machine’s freight.
- The cities off the route have ports as well: rows of lamp-topped pylons and a mirror tower on the Eclipse city’s esplanade, stakes and old mooring ropes round your ship on Salt Harbour’s salt with a mast crane and a sand-skiff, a gantry and couriers on the Waterfall’s ledge, and a rust-orange crane with an ore bucket at the Buried Machine’s freight stage.
- The Underwater City and the Glass Dunes have ports too. On the Dock’s dome a coral pontoon ring with brass rims lies under your ship, with a little cupola and two pods moored, and round pods glide between the domes. In the Glass Dunes your ship stands on a pale stone landing disc in a yard of ochre canvases, racks of green glass floats, float-sledges and a glass skiff.
- The City-Shaft and the Signal Market have space ports round your ship. On the shaft’s rim, lacquered pink and lilac cradles reach out over the drop either side of your way to the edge, a gantry crane lowers a crate into the shaft, and three cabs wait at a stand. In the Market your ship stands on a ruled apron under a tall board of call-signs, one board dark, with a courier, a yellow walker and a hauler parked by it.
- The City Floating in Space’s Pier is a real port now, drawn after its picked design: red bands round the deck, blue iron hoops hung off its rim with a moorer’s boat in three of them, a lamp-mast and the harbour hut by the bridge, and round-bellied sky-boats crossing the dark. It replaces the plain docks of the last version.
- Lorn II’s marsh wisp now wears the colours of its own design sheet: mossy green-black petals patched with pale lichen rosettes, a brass band round its waist in place of the ring of light, plain ink drips, pale green eyes and a cold green marsh-light for a coal.
- The ember wisp has its real body now, drawn from its design sheet: a pointed bud of four glossy black ink petals edged in gold, closed round its glowing coal, with a thin ring of light round its waist, two lilac eyes and three drips of ink hanging beneath. Before it throws its fireball, the petals open wide like a flower round the coal as it swells white-hot. Knocked down by its own fire, it rests just above the ground with its petals hanging open.
- In the Unity version, the camera and the world round you behave as on the web too: the camera stays out of the cabs and carriers instead of passing through them; riding the bike or the bird, the camera takes the ride’s own view, and on the jets it comes round behind and tips with you, pulling back the faster you fly; the benches are there to sit on; the hoverbike and the skiff leave their glowing trails behind them; leaning on a world’s edge, the wind streams in round you and the ink shimmers where you touch it; in the Lab each biome room puts on its own world’s look, planets and hour; a potion’s hearts come back in a warm glow, and the first time you are hurt short of hearts you are told how to drink one; where a world wants to show you the way at once, the drone points it out; and the Start menu counts the fragments of the singing light you have found.
- Inside the makers’ houses the wings now glide heavy: they open as a brake and come down steeply, with a lower, duller flap, so a running jump, the double jump and a glide no longer carry you over a chasm before its bridge or gate is open. Out in the world they glide as before, and moving air still carries them indoors: the columns, the gusts, the Warden’s Well’s draught out to its high ledge, and in the Aerie the breath of the Wind Well, the Eyrie’s gap, the Gulf and the Roost, so their glides work as they did.
- A roll takes you through every hazard of the fight temples unharmed: gusts, millstones, ore carts, falling loads, the eyes’ flash and the toll as well as slag, oil, broken glass, the dark and the shade. Rolling across burning ground no longer flashes as a perfect dodge.
